Properties of Generalized Forchheimer Flows in Porous Media
Luan T. Hoang †,∗, Thinh T. Kieu †, Tuoc V. Phan ‡

Abstract
. The nonlinear Forchheimer equations are used to describe the dynamics of fluid flows in porous media when Darcy’s law is not applicable. In this article, we consider the generalized Forchheimer flows for slightly compressible fluids and study the initial boundary value problem for the resulting degenerate parabolic equation for pressure with the time-dependent flux boundary condition. We estimate L ∞ L^{\infty} -norm for pressure and its time derivative, as well as other Lebesgue norms for its gradient and second spatial derivatives. The asymptotic estimates as time tends to infinity are emphasized. We then show that the solution (in interior L ∞ L^{\infty} -norms) and its gradient (in interior L 2 − δ L^{2-\delta} -norms) depend continuously on the initial and boundary data, and coefficients of the Forchheimer polynomials. These are proved for both finite time intervals and time infinity. The De Giorgi and Ladyzhenskaya-Uraltseva iteration techniques are combined with uniform Gronwall-type estimates, specific monotonicity properties, suitable parabolic Sobolev embeddings and a new fast geometric convergence result.



1.
Introduction

In literature, Darcy’s equation is considered as law of hydrodynamics in porous media. This linear relation between the fluid velocity and pressure gradient was derived by Darcy in
[
7
]
.
However, even in their early works, Darcy and Dupuit already observed deviations of fluid flows from this linear equation
[
7
,
11
]
.
In early 1900s, Forchheimer proposed three models for nonlinear flows, the so-called two-term, three-term and power laws (cf.
[
13
,
14
]
) . More experiments gave rise to more nonlinear models during 1940-60s (cf.
[
2
,
22
]
). Despite that fact, most mathematical papers on fluids in porous media deal only with Darcy’s law starting from 1960s. The mathematical investigations of Forchheimer equations and related Brinkman-Forchheimer equation started much later in 1990s and have been growing ever since (cf.
[
6
,
25
,
24
,
23
,
15
,
4
,
5
,
16
]
, see also
[
29
]
). Even so, most of these papers consider incompressible fluids only. In our previous works
[
1
,
17
,
18
,
19
]
,
we proposed and studied generalized Forchheimer equations for slightly compressible fluids in porous media. Such mathematical generalization is appropriate and useful. This is due to the nature of Forchheimer equations which are derived from experiments and have their physical parameters found by fitting real life data.
From mathematical point of view, it introduces a new class of degenerate parabolic equations into studies of porous medium equations
[
31
]
.
In our mentioned papers, we study the properties of pressure in space
L^{\alpha}
(
1\leq\alpha<\infty
), of pressure gradient in space
L^{2-a}
and of pressure’s time derivative in
L^{2}
. Here
a
is a number between
0
and
1
defined in terms the degree of the polynomial in the generalized Forchheimer equations (see (
2.9
)).
In this paper, we focus on properties of fluid flows in higher regularity spaces.
Specifically, we will study the pressure and its time derivative in space
L^{\infty}
, the pressure gradient in
L^{s}
for any
1\leq s<\infty
and the pressure Hessian in
L^{2-\delta}
for
\delta>0
.
Moreover, our high priority is the long-time dynamical properties, including uniform estimates in time, asymptotic bounds and asymptotic stability.
Such topics of long-time dynamics of degenerate parabolic equation, particularly in
L^{\infty}
, is important, and the specific results are usually hard to obtain.
(See, for e.g.,
[
30
]
, chapters 18–20 of
[
31
]
for porous medium equations,
[
26
,
27
]
for degenerate equations with Dirichlet boundary condition,
[
10
]
for systems.)
In order to work in these much higher regularity spaces and obtain estimates for large time, more sophisticated techniques are called for.
We combine iteration techniques by De Giorgi
[
8
]
and Ladyzhenskaya-Uraltseva
[
20
]
, which were primarily used for studying local properties of solutions to elliptic and parabolic problems, with those from long-time dynamics studies for nonlinear partial differential equations such as Navier-Stokes equations
[
12
]
. For our degenerate equations, we also use and refine relevant techniques in DiBenedetto’s book
[
9
]
.
Such a combination gives fruitful results on the estimates of solutions for large time as well as detailed continuous dependence of the solutions on time-dependent boundary data and coefficients of the Forchheimer polynomials. (The latter results are called structural stability.)
We also emphasize that the mentioned general techniques from parabolic equations must be used in accordance with the structure of our equation, in this case, the important monotonicity and perturbed monotonicity in Lemma
2.2
below.

In the current work, we focus on the case of Degree Condition, see (
DC
) in the next section, for the following reasons.
First, it already covers most commonly used Forchheimer equations in practice, namely, the two-term, three-term and power laws.
Second, to take advantage of available estimates in our previous work
[
18
]
.
Third, to make clear our ideas and techniques without involving much more complicated technical details in case that the Degree Condition is not met (see
[
19
]
); such case will be investigated in our future work.

The paper is organized as follows.
In section
2
, we present the formulation of generalized Forchheimer equations and consequently obtain a degenerate parabolic equation for pressure
p
. Basic properties of this equation are reviewed and suitable parabolic Sobolev embeddings are presented.
In section
3
, we study the initial boundary value problem (IBVP) for pressure with the time-dependent flux boundary data
\psi(x,t)
.
We derive various estimates for the shifted solution
\bar{p}
(see (
3.6
)).
This section is divided into four subsections.
Subsection
3.1
deals with
L^{\infty}
-estimates up to the boundary for
\bar{p}
.
In Theorem
3.4
, bounds are established for all time, large time (independent of initial data) and time infinity.
The precise estimates for both small and large data are achieved by using Lemma
A.2
in the Appendix – a generalization of the classical fast geometric convergence of sequences of numbers.
Subsection
3.2
deals with interior
L^{s}
-estimates for
\nabla p
for any
s\geq 1
, see Theorems
3.10
and
3.12
.
The key Sobolev embedding is Lemma
3.7
with specific weight
K(|\nabla w|)
related to our degenerate structure.
The main iteration step is (
3.46
).
Subsection
3.3
deals with interior
L^{\infty}
-estimates for
\bar{p}_{t}
, where De Giorgi’s technique is applied to the time derivative with weighted parabolic Sobolev embedding.
The main estimates are in Theorem
3.16
, other particular large time and asymptotic estimates are in Theorems
3.17
and
3.18
.
Subsection
3.4
deals with interior
L^{2-\delta}
-estimates with
\delta\in(0,a]
for Hessian
\nabla^{2}p
.
The estimates in Theorems
3.21
,
3.22
and
3.23
for second derivatives were not considered in our previous works.
Sections
4
and
5
are devoted to the structural stability issues.
In section
4
, we prove the continuous dependence of the solutions on the boundary data. Specifically, it is established for
\bar{p}

in interior
L^{\infty}
-norms (see Theorem
4.4
) and for
\nabla p
in interior
L^{2-\delta}
-norms (see Theorems
4.12
and
4.15
for finite time intervals, and Theorems
4.6
,
4.9
and
4.13
for
t\to\infty
).
The results show that even when each individual flux
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
grows unbounded as time
t\to\infty
, the difference between two corresponding solutions
\bar{p}_{1},\bar{p}_{2}
can be small provided the difference
\Psi=\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}
is small.
In order to obtain this, De Giorgi’s iteration is combined, in Proposition
4.1
, with the monotonicity available for our degenerate equation.
In section
5
, we prove the continuous dependence of the solutions on the Forchheimer polynomials (see Theorems
5.3
,
5.4
,
5.7
and
5.9
).
Here, the perturbed monotonicity is combined with De Giorgi’s technique as presented in Proposition
5.1
.
It is proved in Theorems
5.5
and
5.8
that the smallness of the difference
\bar{P}(x,t)
between two solutions corresponding to two Forchheimer equations, when
t\to\infty
, can be controlled by the difference between coefficient vectors of the two Forchheimer polynomials.

2.
Preliminaries

We consider a fluid in a porous medium having velocity
u(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, pressure
p(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}
and density
\rho(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}=[0,\infty)
, with the spatial variable
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and time variable
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
The space dimension
n=3
in physics problems, but here we consider any
n\geq 2
.
Generalized Forchheimer equations, studied in
[
1
,
17
]
, are of the form:

g(|u|)u=-\nabla p,

(2.1)

where
g(s)\geq 0
is a function defined on
[0,\infty)
.
When

g(s)=\alpha,\ \alpha+\beta s,\ \alpha+\beta s+\gamma s^{2},\ \alpha+\gamma_{m}s^{m-1},

where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,m,\gamma_{m}
are empirical constants, we have Darcy’s law, Forchheimer’s two-term, three-term and power laws, respectively.
In this paper, the function
g
in (
2.1
) is a generalized polynomial with non-negative coefficients, that is,

g(s)=a_{0}s^{\alpha_{0}}+a_{1}s^{\alpha_{1}}+\ldots+a_{N}s^{\alpha_{N}},\ s\geq 0,

(2.2)

where

N\geq 1
,
\alpha_{0}=0<\alpha_{1}<\ldots<\alpha_{N}
are real (not necessarily integral) numbers, the coefficients satisfy
a_{0},a_{N}>0
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N-1}\geq 0
.
The number
\alpha_{N}
is the degree of
g
and is denoted by
\deg(g)
.
Denote the vectors of powers and coefficients by
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{N})
and

\vec{a}=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{N})
.
The class of functions
g(s)
as in (
2.2
) is denoted by FP(
N,\vec{\alpha}
), which is the abbreviation of “Forchheimer polynomials”. When the function
g
in (
2.1
) belongs to FP(
N,\vec{\alpha}
), it is referred to as the Forchheimer polynomial.

From relation (
2.1
) one can solve velocity
u
in terms of pressure gradient
\nabla p
and
derives a nonlinear version of Darcy’s equation:

u=-K(|\nabla p|)\nabla p.

(2.3)

The function
K:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is defined by

K(\xi)=\frac{1}{g(s(\xi))},\text{ where }s=s(\xi)\geq 0\text{ satisfies }sg(s)=\xi,\ \text{ for }\xi\geq 0.

(2.4)

We will use notation
g(s,\vec{a})
,
K(\xi,\vec{a})
,
s(\xi,\vec{a})
to denote the corresponding functions in (
2.2
) and (
2.4
) when the dependence on
\vec{a}
needs be specified.

Equation (
2.1
) replaces the momentum equation in fluid mechanics. In addition to this, we have the equation of continuity

\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot(\rho u)=0,

(2.5)

and the equation of state which, for slightly compressible fluids, is

\frac{d\rho}{dp}=\frac{\rho}{\kappa},\quad\kappa>0.

(2.6)

From equations (
2.3
), (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) one derives an equation for the pressure:

\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}=\kappa\nabla\cdot(K(|\nabla p|)\nabla p)+K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2}.

(2.7)

Since the constant
\kappa
is very large for most slightly compressible fluids in porous media
[
21
]
, we neglect the second term on the right-hand side of (
2.7
). This results in the following reduced equation

\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}=\kappa\nabla\cdot(K(|\nabla p|)\nabla p).

(2.8)

Note that this reduction is commonly used in engineering.
By rescaling the time variable, hereafter we assume that
\kappa=1
.

Let
g=g(s,\vec{a})
in FP(
N,\vec{\alpha}
).
The following numbers are in our calculations:

a=\frac{\alpha_{N}}{1+\alpha_{N}}\in(0,1),\ b=\frac{a}{2-a}=\frac{\alpha_{N}}{2+\alpha_{N}}\in(0,1),

(2.9)

\chi(\vec{a})=\max\Big\{a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{N},\frac{1}{a_{0}},\frac{1}{a_{N}}\Big\}\in[1,\infty).

(2.10)

The following properties for
K(\xi,a)
are proved Lemmas III.5 and III.9 of
[
1
]
.
For
\xi\geq 0
, we have

-a\,K(\xi,\vec{a})\leq\xi\,\frac{\partial K(\xi,\vec{a})}{\partial\xi}\leq 0.

(2.11)

Consequently,
K(\xi,\vec{a})
is decreasing in the variable
\xi
and hence

K(\xi,\vec{a})\leq K(0,\vec{a})=a_{0}^{-1}\leq\chi(\vec{a}),

(2.12)

Moreover,
K(\xi,\vec{a})\xi^{m}
is increasing in
\xi
for all
m\geq 1
.

For the type of degeneracy of
K(\xi,\vec{a})
in
\xi
, we recall the following facts.

Lemma 2.1

(cf.
[
17
]
, Lemma 2.1)
.

Let
g(s,\vec{a})
be in class FP(
N,\vec{\alpha}
). One has for any
\xi\geq 0
that

\frac{C_{0}^{-1}\chi(\vec{a})^{-1-a}}{(1+\xi)^{a}}\leq K(\xi,\vec{a})\leq\frac{C_{0}\chi(\vec{a})^{1+a}}{(1+\xi)^{a}},

(2.13)

and for any
m\geq 1
,
\delta>0
that

c_{0}^{-1}\chi(\vec{a})^{-1-a}\frac{\delta^{a}}{(1+\delta)^{a}}(\xi^{m-a}-\delta^{m-a})\leq K(\xi,\vec{a})\xi^{m}\leq c_{0}\chi(\vec{a})^{1+a}\xi^{m-a},

(2.14)

where
c_{0}=c_{0}(N,\alpha_{N})>0
depends on
N
and
\alpha_{N}
only.
In particular, when
m=2
,
\delta=1
, one has

2^{-a}c_{0}^{-1}\chi(\vec{a})^{-1-a}(\xi^{2-a}-1)\leq K(\xi,\vec{a})\xi^{2}\leq c_{0}\chi(\vec{a})^{1+a}\xi^{2-a}.

(2.15)

Same as in
[
1
,
17
]
, we define

H(\xi,\vec{a})=\int_{0}^{\xi^{2}}K(\sqrt{s},\vec{a})ds\quad\hbox{for }\xi\geq 0.

(2.16)

When vector
\vec{a}
is fixed, we denote
K(\cdot,\vec{a})
and
H(\cdot,\vec{a})
by
K(\cdot)
and
H(\cdot)
, respectively.
The function
H(\xi)
can be compared with
\xi
and
K(\xi)
by

K(\xi)\xi^{2}\leq H(\xi)\leq 2K(\xi)\xi^{2},\quad c_{1}(\xi^{2-a}-1)\leq H(\xi)\leq c_{2}\xi^{2-a},

(2.17)

where
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depend on
\chi(\vec{a})
.

Next, we recall important monotonicity properties.
For convenience, we use the following notation: let
\vec{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
and
\vec{x}^{\prime}=(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots)
be two arbitrary vectors of the same length, including possible length
1
. We denote by
\vec{x}\vee\vec{x}^{\prime}
and
\vec{x}\wedge\vec{x}^{\prime}
their maximum and minimum vectors, respectively, with components

(\vec{x}\vee\vec{x}^{\prime})_{j}=\max\{x_{j},x^{\prime}_{j}\}
and
(\vec{x}\wedge\vec{x}^{\prime})_{j}=\min\{x_{j},x^{\prime}_{j}\}
.

Lemma 2.2

(cf.
[
17
]
, Lemma 5.2)
.

Let
g(s,\vec{a})
and
g(s,\vec{a}^{\prime})
belong to class FP(
N,\vec{\alpha}
). Then for any
y,y^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one has

(K(|y|,\vec{a})y-K(|y^{\prime}|,\vec{a}^{\prime})y^{\prime})\cdot(y-y^{\prime})\geq(1-a)\cdot K(|y|\vee|y^{\prime}|,\vec{a}\vee\vec{a}^{\prime})\cdot|y-y^{\prime}|^{2}\\
-N\cdot\max\{\chi(\vec{a}),\chi(\vec{a}^{\prime})\}\cdot|\vec{a}-\vec{a}^{\prime}|\cdot K(|y|\vee|y^{\prime}|,\vec{a}\wedge\vec{a}^{\prime})\cdot(|y|\vee|y^{\prime}|)\cdot|y-y^{\prime}|,

(2.18)

where
a\in(0,1)
is defined in (
2.9
). Particularly, when
\vec{a}=\vec{a}^{\prime}
, we have

(K(|y|,\vec{a})y-K(|y^{\prime}|,\vec{a})y^{\prime})\cdot(y-y^{\prime})\geq(1-a)\cdot K(|y|\vee|y^{\prime}|,\vec{a})\cdot|y-y^{\prime}|^{2}.

(2.19)

When the fluid is confined in an open, bounded domain
U
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the Sobolev embeddings play an important role in our study. For
0<r<n
, we denote by
r^{*}
the critical Sobolev exponent, i.e.
r^{*}=\frac{nr}{n-r}
. The following
fact is used frequently

r^{*}>2\Leftrightarrow\frac{nr}{n-r}>2\Leftrightarrow r>\frac{2n}{n+2}\Leftrightarrow(2-r)n<2r.

(2.20)

With this notation, we define the
Degree Condition
as one of the following equivalent conditions:

\deg(g)\leq\frac{4}{n-2},\quad a\leq\frac{4}{n+2},\quad 2\leq(2-a)^{*},\quad 2-a\geq\frac{2n}{n+2}.

(
DC
)

Similarly, we define the
Strict Degree Condition
as one of the following equivalent conditions:

\deg(g)<\frac{4}{n-2},\quad a<\frac{4}{n+2},\quad 2<(2-a)^{*},\quad 2-a>\frac{2n}{n+2}.

(
SDC
)

We will assume the Strict Degree Condition very often in this paper, but not always. Whenever this condition is met, the Sobolev space
W^{1,2-a}(U)
is continuously embedded into
L^{2}(U)
.

We now consider parabolic Sobolev embeddings.
Let us denote throughout
Q_{T}=U\times(0,T)
.

Lemma 2.3
.

If
2n/(n+2)\leq r\leq 2
,
r<n
and
p=r(n+2)/n
then

\|u\|_{L^{p}(Q_{T})}\leq C(1+T^{1/p})[[u]]_{2,r;T},

(2.21)

where
C=C(U,n,r)>0
is independent of
T
and

[[u]]_{2,r;T}=\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{r}(Q_{T})}.

(2.22)

In (
2.21
) above, we can remove
T^{1/p}
whenever
u
vanishes on the boundary of
U
.

Proof.

The proof is standard, cf.
[
20
,
9
]
.
The short proof is presented here for the sake of completeness, and also serves the next lemma.
For convenience we denote
[[\cdot]]\equiv[[\cdot]]_{2,r,T}
.
Note that
2\leq p\leq r^{*}
. We write

\frac{1}{p}=\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{\beta}{r^{*}},\quad\alpha,\beta\geq 0,\quad\alpha+\beta=1.

(2.23)

By interpolation inequality and Sobolev embedding, we have

\|u(t)\|_{L^{p}(U)}\leq\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{\alpha}\|u(t)\|_{L^{r^{*}}(U)}^{\beta}\leq C\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{\alpha}(\delta\|u(t)\|_{L^{r}(U)}^{\beta}+\|{\nabla u(t)}\|_{L^{r}(U)}^{\beta}),

where
\delta=1
in general, and
\delta=0
in case
u
vanishes on the boundary
\partial U
.
Note that
r\leq 2
then
\|u(t)\|_{L^{r}(U)}\leq C\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(U)}
. Thus,

\|u(t)\|_{L^{p}(U)}\leq C\delta\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(U)}+C\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(U)}^{\alpha}\|{\nabla u(t)}\|_{L^{r}(U)}^{\beta}.

(2.24)

Raising (
2.24
) to power
p
, integrating it in
t
from
0
to
T
and using the fact that

\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(U)}\leq[[u]]
a.e. in
[0,T]
, we obtain

\|{u}\|_{L^{p}(Q_{T})}^{p}\leq C\delta T[[u]]^{p}+C[[u]]^{\alpha p}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{r}(U)}^{\beta p}dt.

(2.25)

From (
2.23
), we find
\beta=\frac{n}{n+2}
. Thus,
\beta p=r
and we have from (
2.25
) that

\displaystyle\|{u}\|_{L^{p}(Q_{T})}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\delta T[[u]]^{p}+C[[u]]^{\alpha p}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\nabla u(x,t)|^{r}dxdt\Big)^{\frac{\beta p}{r}}

(2.26)

\displaystyle\leq C\delta T[[u]]^{p}+C[[u]]^{\alpha p}[[u]]^{\beta p}=C[[u]]^{p}(\delta T+1).

Therefore, we obtain (
2.21
).
∎

The following parabolic Sobolev embedding with spatial weights will be useful in this paper.

Lemma 2.4
.

Given
W(x,t)>0
on
Q_{T}
. Suppose two numbers
m
and
r
satisfy
2n/(n+2)\leq r\leq 2
,
r<n
and
r<m<r^{*}
.
Let

p=2+m-\frac{2m}{r^{*}}.

(2.27)

Then

\|u\|_{L^{p}(Q_{T})}\leq C[[u]]_{2,m,W;T}\Big\{T^{1/p}+\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{t\in[0,T]}\Big(\int_{U}W(x,t)^{-\frac{r}{m-r}}dx\Big)^{\frac{m-r}{pr}}\Big\},

(2.28)

where

[[u]]_{2,m,W;T}=\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{t\in[0,T]}\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}W(x,t)|\nabla u|^{m}dxdt\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}.

(2.29)

Consequently, under the Strict Degree Condition, when
m=2
and
r=2-a
we have

\|u\|_{L^{p}(Q_{T})}\leq C[[u]]_{2,2,W;T}\Big\{T^{1/p}+\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{t\in[0,T]}\Big(\int_{U}W(x,t)^{-\frac{2-a}{a}}dx\Big)^{\frac{2}{p(2-a)}}\Big\},

(2.30)

where
2<p<(2-a)^{*}
given explicitly by

p=4\Big(1-\frac{1}{(2-a)^{*}}\Big).

(2.31)

In (
2.28
) and (
2.30
) above, we can remove
T^{1/p}
whenever
u
vanishes on the boundary of
U
.

Proof.

In this proof we denote
[[\cdot]]=[[\cdot]]_{2,m,W;T}
.
By Hölder’s inequality,

\|\nabla u\|_{L^{r}(U)}=\Big(\int_{U}W^{\frac{r}{m}}|\nabla u|^{r}\cdot W^{-\frac{r}{m}}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{r}}\leq\Big(\int_{U}W|\nabla u|^{m}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\Big(\int_{U}W^{-\frac{r}{m-r}}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{m}}.

(2.32)

By (
2.20
) and the condition on
r
we have
r^{*}\geq 2
. With exponent
p
in (
2.27
), we write

p-2=\frac{m(r^{*}-2)}{r^{*}},\quad p-r^{*}=\frac{(r^{*}-2)(m-r^{*})}{r^{*}},\quad p-m=\frac{2(r^{*}-m)}{r^{*}},

(2.33)

then we have
2\leq p\leq r^{*}
and
p>m
.
Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be defined as in (
2.23
).
Then applying (
2.24
) and (
2.32
) yields

\|{u}\|_{L^{p}(Q_{T})}^{p}\leq C\delta T[[u]]^{p}+C[[u]]^{\alpha p}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\int_{U}W|\nabla u|^{m}dx\Big)^{\frac{\beta p}{m}}d\tau\Big]\\
\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{[0,T]}\Big(\int_{U}W(x,t)^{-\frac{r}{m-r}}dx\Big)^{\beta p(\frac{1}{r}-\frac{1}{m})}.

(2.34)

From (
2.23
) and (
2.33
), we have

\beta=\frac{r^{*}(p-2)}{p(r^{*}-2)}=\frac{m}{p}.

Therefore, we rewrite (
2.34
) as

\displaystyle\|{u}\|_{L^{p}(Q_{T})}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\delta T[[u]]^{p}+C[[u]]^{\alpha p}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}W|\nabla u|^{m}dxd\tau\Big)^{\frac{\beta p}{m}}\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{[0,T]}\Big(\int_{U}W(x,t)^{-\frac{r}{m-r}}dx\Big)^{\frac{m-r}{r}}

\displaystyle\leq C\delta T[[u]]^{p}+C[[u]]^{\alpha p}[[u]]^{\beta p}\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{[0,T]}\Big(\int_{U}W(x,t)^{-\frac{r}{m-r}}dx\Big)^{\frac{m-r}{r}}

\displaystyle\leq C[[u]]^{p}\Big\{\delta T+\mathop{\mathrm{ess\,sup}}_{[0,T]}\Big(\int_{U}W(x,t)^{-\frac{r}{m-r}}dx\Big)^{\frac{m-r}{r}}\Big\}.

Thus, we obtain (
2.28
).
In particular, when
m=2
and
r=2-a
then (
2.27
) becomes (
2.31
).
Under the Strict Degree Condition, requirements on
r
and
m
are met.
Then (
2.30
) follows (
2.28
).
∎

Another type of embedding will be proved in Lemma
3.7
.

3.
Estimates of solutions

Let
U
be a bounded, open, connected subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with boundary
\Gamma
of class
C^{2}
.
We consider a fluid flow in
U
that satisfies the generalized Forchheimer
equation (
2.1
) with a fixed
g(s)=g(s,\vec{a})\in FP(N,\vec{\alpha})
.
We study the resulting parabolic equation for the pressure
p=p(x,t)
:

\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}=\nabla\cdot(K(|\nabla p|)\nabla p),\quad x\in U,\ t>0.

(3.1)

In addition, we assume the flux condition on the boundary:

u\cdot\vec{\nu}=\psi(x,t),\quad x\in\Gamma,\ t>0,

where
\vec{\nu}
is the outward normal vector on
\Gamma
and the flux
\psi(x,t)
is known. Hence, by (
2.3
) we have

-K(|\nabla p|)\nabla p\cdot\vec{\nu}=\psi\quad\hbox{on}\quad\Gamma\times(0,\infty).

(3.2)

The initial data

p(x,0)=p_{0}(x)\text{ is given.}

(3.3)

We will focus on the IBVP (
3.1
), (
3.2
) and (
3.3
). By integrating (
3.1
) over
U
, we easily find

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}p(x,t)dx=\int_{\Gamma}K(|\nabla p|)\nabla p\cdot\vec{\nu}d\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}\psi(x,t)d\sigma,\quad t>0.

(Here
d\sigma
is the surface area element.)
By the continuity of
\int_{U}p(x,t)dx
and
\int_{\Gamma}\psi(x,t)d\sigma
on
[0,\infty)
, we assert

\int_{U}p(x,t)dx=\int_{U}p(x,0)dx-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\psi(x,\tau)d\sigma d\tau,\quad t\geq 0.

(3.4)

Let
\bar{p}(x,t)=p(x,t)-\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{U}p(x,t)dx
, for
x\in U
and
t\geq 0
, where
|U|
denotes the volume of
U
.
Then
\bar{p}
satisfies the zero average condition

\int_{U}\bar{p}(x,t)dx=0\quad\hbox{for all }t\geq 0.

(3.5)

It follows from (
3.4
) that for
t\geq 0
,

\bar{p}(x,t)=p(x,t)-\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{U}p_{0}(x)dx+\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\psi(x,\tau)d\sigma d\tau.

(3.6)

We call
\bar{p}(x,t)
the shifted solution.
Then
\bar{p}
satisfies the following IBVP

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}&\dfrac{\partial\bar{p}}{\partial t}=\nabla\cdot(K(|\nabla\bar{p}|)\nabla\bar{p})+\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{\Gamma}\psi(x,t)d\sigma,&\quad x\in U,t>0,\\
&-K(|\nabla\bar{p}|)\nabla\bar{p}\cdot\vec{\nu}=\psi(x,t),&\quad x\in\Gamma,t>0,\\
&\bar{p}(x,0)=p_{0}(x)-\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{U}p_{0}(x)dx,&\quad x\in U.\end{array}\right.

(3.7)

Note that even in the linear case, i.e.,
K(\xi)\equiv const.
and
\psi(x,t)
is uniformly bounded on
\Gamma\times[0,\infty)
, the solution
p(x,t)
can still be unbounded as
t\to\infty
.
Therefore instead of estimating
p(x,t)
directly, we will estimate
\bar{p}(x,t)
.
Thanks to the explicit relation (
3.6
) between
p
and
\bar{p}
, the obtained results will have clear physical interpretations and the estimates for
p(x,t)
can be easily retrieved from those for
\bar{p}(x,t)
.

The existence of weak solutions can be treated by the theory of nonlinear monotone operators (cf.
[
3
,
28
,
32
]
, see also section 3 of
[
19
]
for our proof for the Dirichlet boundary condition). The regularity of weak solutions is treated in
[
9
]
.
For simplicity, we always assume that the initial data
p_{0}
and boundary data
\psi
are sufficiently smooth and the solution of (
3.7
) exists
for all

t\geq 0
. Also, sufficient regularity of the solution is assumed.
For example, when we estimate
L^{\infty}
-norm of
\bar{p}
we require
\bar{p}\in C(\bar{U}\times[0,\infty))
;
when we estimate
L^{\infty}
-norm of
\bar{p}_{t}
we require
\bar{p}_{t}\in C(U\times(0,\infty))
;
when we estimate
L^{s}
-norm of
\nabla p
we require
\nabla p\in C([0,\infty),L_{loc}^{s}(U))
.

In the following subsections, we derive various estimates for pressure and its derivatives.
These estimates are important by themselves and for the next sections when we study the stability of the solutions.

Notation
.

In estimates below, constants
C
’s always depend on the dimension
n
, domain
U
and the Forchheimer polynomial
g(s,\vec{a})
. Additional dependence will be specified as needed.

We use short-hand notation
\|f\|_{L^{2}}
,
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}
for the norms
\|f\|_{L^{2}(U)}
,
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}
whenever
f
is defined on
U
. Similarly, if a function
\phi
is defined on
\Gamma
, we use short-hand notation

\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}
,
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}
for the norms
\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}
,
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}
.

If
f(x,t)
is a function of two variables, we denote by
f(t)
the function
t\to f(\cdot,t)
, therefore
\|f(t)\|_{L^{2}}
means
\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}
and
\sup_{[0,T]}\|f\|_{L^{2}}
means
\sup\{\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}:t\in[0,T]\}
.

Throughout, we denote
Q_{T}=U\times(0,T)
.
Also, we use the following notation for partial derivatives:
\partial p/\partial t=p_{t}
and
\partial/\partial x_{i}=\partial_{i}
.

3.1.
Estimates for pressure

We begin our subsection by introducing the following result on the
L^{\infty}
-estimates of for the
\bar{p}
of the IBVP (
3.7
).
Throughout the paper, we denote

{\mu_{1}}=(2-a)(n+2)/n.

(3.8)

Proposition 3.1
.

Assume (
SDC
). Then,
there is a constant
C>0
such that any
T>0
, the following inequality holds

\sup_{[0,T]}\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big\{\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+(1+T)^{\frac{2}{2-a}}(\sup_{[0,T]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}+1)^{\frac{1}{1-a}}\Big\}.

(3.9)

Proof.

We follow the celebrated De Giorgi’s technique. Although, the method is standard (see, for example,
[
20
]
), calculations are tedious and new. Thus, for completeness, we provide its details here. For any
k\geq 0
, let us denote

\displaystyle\bar{p}^{(k)}=\max\{\bar{p}-k,0\},\quad S_{k}(t)=\{x\in U:\bar{p}^{(k)}(x,t)\geq 0\},\quad\sigma_{k}=\int_{0}^{T}|S_{k}(t)|dt,

\displaystyle F_{k}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}(x,t)|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}(x,t)|^{2-a}dxdt.

Assume
k\geq\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}
, then
\bar{p}_{0}^{(k)}(x)=0
a.e..
Multiplying the first equation of (
3.7
) by
\bar{p}^{(k)}
and integrating
the resultant over the domain
U
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx+\int_{U}K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx=\int_{\Gamma}\psi\bar{p}^{(k)}d\sigma+\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{\Gamma}\psi(x,t)d\sigma\int_{U}\bar{p}^{(k)}dx.

We bound
|\psi(x,t)|
and
|\int_{\Gamma}\psi(x,t)d\sigma|
by
C\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}
, and apply the trace theorem to the boundary integral, and then Hölder’s inequality to have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx+\int_{U}K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx\leq C\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{U}\Big(|\bar{p}^{(k)}|+|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|\Big)dx

\displaystyle\leq C\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\{\Big(\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx\Big)^{1/2}|S_{k}(t)|^{1/2}+\Big(\int_{S_{k}(t)}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{2-a}}|S_{k}(t)|^{\frac{1-a}{2-a}}\right\}.

Using (
2.15
) to compare
|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}
with
K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}+1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx+\int_{U}K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx\leq C\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big\{\Big(\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx\Big)^{1/2}|S_{k}(t)|^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\Big(\int_{S_{k}(t)}K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{2-a}}|S_{k}(t)|^{\frac{1-a}{2-a}}+|S_{k}(t)|\Big\}.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. By Young’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{U}K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx\leq\varepsilon\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad\quad+C\Big\{\varepsilon^{-1}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big\}|S_{k}(t)|.

Note that
p^{(k)}(0)=0
. For each
t\in[0,T)
, integrating the previous estimate on
(0,t)
, and the taking the supremum in
t
yield

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq 4\varepsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx+C\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\varepsilon^{-1}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big)|S_{k}(t)|dt

\displaystyle\leq 4\varepsilon T\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx+C\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\varepsilon^{-1}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big)|S_{k}(t)|dt.

By taking
\varepsilon=1/(8T)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\left[T\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\right]|S_{k}(t)|dt

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\left[T\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}+1\right]|S_{k}(t)|dt.

Again, using (
2.15
) to compare
K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}
with
|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}
and using Young’s inequality,
one gets

F_{k}\mathbin{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbin{=\kern-2.0pt=}}\limits^{\rm def}}}\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}dxdt\leq C_{0}\alpha_{T}\sigma_{k},

(3.10)

where
C_{0}
is a constant depending on
a
,
n
and
U
, and
\alpha_{T}=T\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}+1
.

We now iterate (
3.10
) to derive our desired estimate. Under the Strict Degree Condition (
SDC
),
{\mu_{1}}>2
, hence, the parabolic Sobolev embedding in Lemma
2.3
with
r=2-a
implies

\|\bar{p}^{(k)}\|_{L^{\mu_{1}}(Q_{T})}\leq C(1+T)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}(F_{k}^{1/2}+F_{k}^{1/(2-a)}).

(3.11)

Next, let
M_{0}\geq\|\bar{p}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{\infty}}
be a fixed number which will be determined.
For each
i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, let
k_{i}=M_{0}(2-2^{-i})
.
Then
k_{i}
is increasing in
i
, and therefore
S_{k_{i}}
,
\sigma_{k_{i}}
are decreasing.
Also, note that
k_{i}\geq M_{0}\geq\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}
for all
i\geq 0
.
We now define
\mathcal{Q}_{k}=\{(x,t)\in U\times(0,T):\bar{p}(x,t)>k\}
.

From (
3.11
) and the fact that

\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{\mu_{1}}(Q_{T})}\geq\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{\mu_{1}}(\mathcal{Q}_{k_{i+1}})}\geq\|{k_{i+1}-k_{i}}\|_{L^{\mu_{1}}(\mathcal{Q}_{k_{i+1}})}\geq(k_{i+1}-k_{i})\sigma_{k_{i+1}}^{1/{\mu_{1}}},

(3.12)

it follows

\sigma_{k_{i+1}}^{1/{\mu_{1}}}\leq\frac{C(1+T)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}}{k_{i+1}-k_{i}}\Big[F_{k_{i}}^{1/2}+F_{k_{i}}^{1/(2-a)}\Big].

This together with (
3.10
) yield

\sigma_{k_{i+1}}^{1/{\mu_{1}}}\leq C(1+T)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}\frac{2^{i}}{M_{0}}\Big[(\alpha_{T}\sigma_{k_{i}})^{1/2}+(\alpha_{T}\sigma_{k_{i}})^{1/(2-a)}\Big].

Equivalently,

\sigma_{k_{i+1}}\leq\frac{C(1+T)\alpha_{T}^{{\mu_{1}}/(2-a)}}{M_{0}^{\mu_{1}}}2^{{\mu_{1}}i}(\sigma_{k_{i}}^{{\mu_{1}}/2}+\sigma_{k_{i}}^{{\mu_{1}}/(2-a)}).

Let us denote
Y_{i}=\sigma_{k_{i}}
,
D=\frac{C(1+T)\alpha_{T}^{{\mu_{1}}/(2-a)}}{M_{0}^{\mu_{1}}}
,
{\theta_{1}}={\mu_{1}}/(2-a)-1>0
and
{\theta_{2}}={\mu_{1}}/2-1>0
. We now obtain

Y_{i+1}\leq D2^{{\mu_{1}}i}[Y_{i}^{1+{\theta_{1}}}+Y_{i}^{1+{\theta_{2}}}].

Note that
k_{0}=M_{0}\geq\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}
and

Y_{0}=\sigma_{M_{0}}\leq|Q_{T}|=|U|T\leq|U|(T+1).

To apply (
A.5
) with
m=2
in Lemma
A.2
, we choose
M_{0}
sufficiently large such that

Y_{0}\leq C\min\{D^{-1/{\theta_{2}}},D^{-1/{\theta_{1}}}\},\quad\text{or sufficiently,}

\displaystyle|U|(T+1)\leq C\min\Big\{((1+T)\alpha_{T}^{{\mu_{1}}/(2-a)}M_{0}^{-{\mu_{1}}})^{-1/{\theta_{2}}},((1+T)\alpha_{T}^{{\mu_{1}}/(2-a)}M_{0}^{-{\mu_{1}}})^{-1/{\theta_{1}}}\Big\}.

It suffices to have

\begin{split}&M_{0}\geq C(1+T)^{\frac{1+{\theta_{2}}}{{\mu_{1}}}}\alpha_{T}^{1/(2-a)}=C(1+T)^{\frac{1}{2}}\alpha_{T}^{\frac{1}{2-a}}\quad\text{and}\\
&M_{0}\geq C(1+T)^{\frac{1+{\theta_{1}}}{{\mu_{1}}}}\alpha_{T}^{1/(2-a)}=C(1+T)^{\frac{1}{2-a}}\alpha_{T}^{\frac{1}{2-a}}.\end{split}

These and the initial requirement
M_{0}\geq\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}
are satisfied if
we choose

M_{0}\geq C\Big\{\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+(1+T)^{\frac{1}{2-a}}\alpha_{T}^{\frac{1}{2-a}}\Big\}.

Note that

(1+T)\alpha_{T}\leq C(T+1)^{2}(\sup_{[0,T]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}+1)^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}
.
Therefore we finally select

M_{0}=C_{1}\Big\{\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+(1+T)^{\frac{2}{2-a}}(\sup_{[0,T]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}+1)^{\frac{1}{1-a}}\Big\}.

Now, applying (
A.5
) in Lemma
A.2
, we have
\sigma_{2M_{0}}=\displaystyle{\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}Y_{i}}=0
.
Thus,
\bar{p}(x,t)\leq 2M_{0}
in
Q_{T}
. Note that we used the continuity of
\bar{p}(x,t)
on
\bar{Q}_{T}
.
By using the same argument with
p
replaced by
-p
and
\psi
replaced by
-\psi
, one can show that
-\bar{p}\leq 2M_{0}
in
Q_{T}
.
Therefore, (
3.9
) follows and the proof is complete.
∎

We emphasize that the
L^{\infty}
-estimate of
\bar{p}
in Proposition
3.1
depends on
L^{\infty}
-norm of the initial data and on
T
. In the next proposition, we give a
L^{\infty}
-estimate which removes the former dependence. The result will be used flexibly to derive the time-uniform
L^{\infty}
-estimate of
\bar{p}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Assume (
SDC
).
Then, there is a constant
C>0
such that for any
T_{0}\geq 0
,
T>0
,
\delta\in(0,1]
and
\theta\in(0,1)
, the following inequality holds

\sup_{[T_{0}+\theta T,T_{0}+T]}\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big\{\sqrt{\mathcal{E}}+(T+1)^{\frac{n}{4-(n+2)a}}\Big(1+\frac{1}{\delta^{a}\theta T}\Big)^{\frac{n+2}{4-(n+2)a}}\\
\cdot\Big(\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(T_{0},T_{0}+T))}+\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(T_{0},T_{0}+T))}^{\frac{4}{4-(n+2)a}}\Big)\Big\},

(3.13)

where

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{T_{0},T}\mathbin{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbin{=\kern-2.0pt=}}\limits^{\rm def}}}\delta^{2-a}+T\delta^{-a}\sup_{[T_{0},T_{0}+T]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\delta^{-\frac{a(2-a)}{1-a}}\sup_{[T_{0},T_{0}+T]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
T_{0}=0
. Again, we use De Giorgi’s iteration technique. But, unlike in the previous one, we will iterate using the
L^{2}
-norm of
\bar{p}
. The proof is therefore significantly different from that of Proposition
3.1
and we give it here in full details.

For each
k\geq 0
, let
\bar{p}^{(k)}
be as
in the proof of the previous theorem. Also, let
\zeta=\zeta(t)
be a non-negative cut-off function on
[0,T]

with
\zeta(0)=0
. Multiplying (
3.7
) by
\bar{p}^{(k)}\zeta
and integrating over
U
, we have

\frac{1}{2}\int_{U}\frac{\partial|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}}{\partial t}\zeta dx+\int_{U}K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta dx=\int_{\Gamma}\psi\bar{p}^{(k)}\zeta d\sigma+\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{\Gamma}\psi(x,t)d\sigma\int_{U}\bar{p}^{(k)}\zeta dx.

(3.14)

Denote by
\chi_{k}(x,t)
the characteristic function of the set
\{(x,t)\in\in U\times(0,T):\bar{p}^{(k)}(x,t)>0\}
.
Then, using (
2.14
) in Lemma
2.1
to estimate
K(|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|)|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\geq C\delta^{a}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}-C\delta^{2}
, and estimating the right-hand side of (
3.14
) the same way as in the proof of Proposition
3.1
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta_{t}dx+C\delta^{a}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}\zeta dx-C\delta^{2}\int_{U}\chi_{k}\zeta dx

\displaystyle\leq C\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{U}(|\bar{p}^{(k)}|\zeta+|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|\zeta)dx.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then it follows from Young’s inequality that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta dx+C_{1}\delta^{a}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}\zeta dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta_{t}dx+C\delta^{2}\int_{U}\chi_{k}(t)\zeta dx+\varepsilon\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta dx+C\varepsilon^{-1}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{U}\chi_{k}\zeta dx

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C_{1}\delta^{a}}{2}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}\zeta dx+C\delta^{-\frac{a}{1-a}}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}\int_{U}\chi_{k}\zeta dx.

Now, integrating this inequality on
(0,t)
and taking supremum of the resultant on
[0,T]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta dx+\frac{C_{1}\delta^{a}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}\zeta dxdt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta_{t}dxdt+2\varepsilon T\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta dx

\displaystyle\quad\quad+C\Big[\delta^{2}+\varepsilon^{-1}\|\psi\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}(0,T;L_{x}^{\infty})}^{2}+\delta^{-\frac{a}{1-a}}\|\psi\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}(0,T;L_{x}^{\infty})}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}\Big]\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}\chi_{k}\zeta dxdt.

By choosing
\varepsilon=1/(8T)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2-a}\zeta dxdt

(3.15)

\displaystyle\leq C\delta^{-a}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta_{t}dxdt+C\mathcal{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}\chi_{k}\zeta dxdt,

where

\mathcal{E}=\delta^{2-a}+T\delta^{-a}\sup_{[0,T]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\delta^{-\frac{a(2-a)}{1-a}}\sup_{[0,T]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}.

We will iterate this relation with
k=k_{i+1}
and
\zeta=\zeta_{i}
. The choice of
k_{i}
and
\zeta_{i}(t)
is as follows.
Let
t_{i}=\theta T(1-2^{-i})
for all
i\geq 0
, and let
\zeta_{i}
be a piecewise linear function with

\zeta_{i}(t)=0
for
t\leq t_{i}
,
\zeta_{i}(t)=1
for
t\geq t_{i+1}
and

|\zeta_{it}(t)|\leq\frac{1}{t_{i+1}-t_{i}}=\frac{2^{i+1}}{\theta T}\quad\forall\ t\in[0,T].

Next, let
M_{0}
be a fixed positive number which will be determined, and let
k_{i}=M_{0}(1-2^{-i})
, for
i\geq 0
.
Note that, because of our choices,
k_{0}=0
and
k_{i+1}-k_{i}=2^{-i-1}M_{0}
and
t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\ldots<\theta T
.
We also denote

A_{i,j}=\{(x,t):u(x,t)>k_{i},\ t\in(t_{j},T)\}
and
A_{i}=A_{i,i}
.
Then, by applying (
3.15
) with
k=k_{i+1}
and
\zeta=\zeta_{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}|^{2}\zeta_{i}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}|^{2-a}\zeta_{i}dxdt

(3.16)

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-a}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}|^{2}(\zeta_{i})_{t}dxdt+C\mathcal{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}\chi_{k_{i+1}}\zeta_{i}dxdt\Big).

Now, we define

F_{i}=\sup_{[t_{i+1},T]}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}|^{2}\zeta_{i}dx+\int_{t_{i}}^{T}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}|^{2-a}\zeta_{i}dxdt.

Then it follows from (
3.16
) that

F_{i}\leq\delta^{-a}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}|^{2}(\zeta_{i})_{t}dxdt+C\mathcal{E}\Big(\int_{t_{i+1}}^{T}\int_{U}\chi_{k_{i+1}}dxdt\Big).

Therefore,

F_{i}\leq C2^{i}(\theta T)^{-1}\delta^{-a}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i+1,i})}^{2}+C\mathcal{E}|A_{i+1,i}|.

(3.17)

Since

\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}\geq\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i+1,i})}\geq(k_{i+1}-k_{i})|A_{i+1,i}|^{1/2}
,
we see that

|A_{i+1,i}|\leq(k_{i+1}-k_{i})^{-2}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}^{2}=4^{i}M_{0}^{-2}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}^{2}.

(3.18)

From (
3.17
) and (
3.18
), we get

F_{i}\leq C\delta^{-a}(\theta T)^{-1}2^{i}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i+1,i})}^{2}+\mathcal{E}4^{i}M_{0}^{-2}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}^{2}\leq CC_{\delta}4^{i}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}^{2},

(3.19)

where

C_{\delta}=\delta^{-a}(\theta T)^{-1}+\mathcal{E}M_{0}^{-2}.

Under the Strict Degree Condition,
2<{\mu_{1}}<(2-a)^{*}
, then applying Lemma
2.3
with
r=2-a
yields

\displaystyle\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}\|_{L^{\mu_{1}}(A_{i+1,i+1})}=\Big(\int_{t_{i+1}}^{T}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}|^{\mu_{1}}dxdt\Big)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+(T-t_{i+1})^{1/{\mu_{1}}})\Big[\sup_{[t_{i+1},T]}\|{\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}}\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\Big(\int_{t_{i+1}}^{T}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}|^{2-a}dx)dt\Big)^{1/(2-a)}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C(1+T)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}\Big[\sup_{[t_{i+1},T]}\|{\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}\zeta_{i}}\|_{L^{2}(U)}+\Big(\int_{t_{i+1}}^{T}\int_{U}|\nabla\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}|^{2-a}\zeta_{i}dxdt\Big)^{1/(2-a)}\Big].

Since
t_{i}\leq t_{i+1}
, it follows that

\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}\|_{L^{\mu_{1}}(A_{i+1,i+1})}\leq C(T+1)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}(F_{i}^{1/2}+F_{i}^{1/(2-a)}).

(3.20)

Then, it follows from Hölder’s inequality and (
3.20
) that

\displaystyle\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i+1,i+1})}\leq\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}\|_{L^{\mu_{1}}(A_{i+1,i+1})}|A_{i+1,i+1}|^{1/2-1/{\mu_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}\|_{L^{\mu_{1}}(A_{i+1,i+1})}|A_{i+1,i}|^{1/2-1/{\mu_{1}}}\leq C(T+1)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}(F_{i}^{1/2}+F_{i}^{1/(2-a)})|A_{i+1,i}|^{1/2-1/{\mu_{1}}}.

This and (
3.18
), (
3.19
) give

\displaystyle\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i+1})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i+1,i+1})}

\displaystyle\leq 4^{i}C(T+1)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}\Big(C_{\delta}^{1/2}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}+C_{\delta}^{1/(2-a)}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}^{2/(2-a)}\Big)M_{0}^{-1+2/{\mu_{1}}}\|p^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}^{1-2/{\mu_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq 4^{i}C(T+1)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}M_{0}^{-1+2/{\mu_{1}}}\Big(C_{\delta}^{1/2}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}^{2-2/{\mu_{1}}}+C_{\delta}^{1/(2-a)}\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}^{1-2/{\mu_{1}}+2/(2-a)}\Big).

Now, let
Y_{i}=\|\bar{p}^{(k_{i})}\|_{L^{2}(A_{i})}
, we get

Y_{i+1}\leq D4^{i}\Big(Y_{i}^{2-2/{\mu_{1}}}+Y_{i}^{1-2/{\mu_{1}}+2/(2-a)}\Big)=D4^{i}\Big(Y_{i}^{1+\mu_{2}}+Y_{i}^{1+\mu_{3}}\Big),

(3.21)

where
D=C(T+1)^{1/{\mu_{1}}}\big(C_{\delta}^{1/2}+C_{\delta}^{1/(2-a)}\big)M_{0}^{-\mu_{2}}
, and

\mu_{2}=1-\frac{2}{\mu_{1}}=\frac{4-a(n+2)}{(2-a)(n+2)},\quad\mu_{3}=2\Big(\frac{1}{2-a}-\frac{1}{{\mu_{1}}}\Big)=\frac{4}{(2-a)(n+2)}.

(3.22)

To be able to apply (
A.5
) with
m=2
in Lemma
A.2
, we will choose
M_{0}
sufficiently large such that

Y_{0}\leq C\min\{D^{-1/\mu_{2}},D^{-1/\mu_{3}}\}.

(3.23)

Since
Y_{0}\leq\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(0,T))}
, it suffices to choose
M_{0}
such that

M_{0}\geq C(T+1)^{\frac{1}{{\mu_{1}}\mu_{2}}}(C_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}+C_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2-a}})^{\frac{1}{\mu_{2}}}\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(0,T))},M_{0}\geq C(T+1)^{\frac{1}{{\mu_{1}}\mu_{2}}}(C_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}+C_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2-a}})^{\frac{1}{\mu_{2}}}\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(0,T))}^{\mu_{3}/\mu_{2}}.

Observe that, if
M_{0}\geq\sqrt{\mathcal{E}}
then
C_{\delta}\leq 1+\frac{1}{\delta^{a}\theta T}\in(1,\infty)
. Thus, (
3.23
) holds for

M_{0}=C\Big\{\sqrt{\mathcal{E}}+(T+1)^{\frac{1}{{\mu_{1}}\mu_{2}}}\big(1+\frac{1}{\delta^{a}\theta T}\big)^{\frac{1}{(2-a)\mu_{2}}}\Big(\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(0,T))}+\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(0,T))}^{\mu_{3}/\mu_{2}}\Big)\Big\}.

With this choice of
M_{0}
, applying (
A.5
) with
m=2
in Lemma
A.2
to (
3.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\lim_{i\to\infty}}Y_{i}=0
. Consequently,

\int_{\theta T}^{T}\int_{U}|\bar{p}^{(M_{0})}|^{2}dxdt=0.

This implies
\bar{p}(x,t)\leq M_{0}
in
U\times(\theta T,T)
.
To prove the lower bound
\bar{p}\geq-M_{0}
, we replace
p
by
-p
and
\psi
by
-\psi
and
use the same argument. We conclude

|\bar{p}(x,t)|\leq M_{0}\quad\text{in }U\times(\theta T,T).

The proof of (
3.13
) is complete.
∎

Our next goal is to combine Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
with
L^{2}
-estimates of
\bar{p}
in
[
18
]

to derive the uniform (in time)
L^{\infty}
-estimate of
\bar{p}
. For our purpose, we need to introduce some notation.
Firstly, let us define two functions
f(t)
and
\tilde{f}(t)
for
t\geq 0
by

\begin{split}f(t)&=\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{f}(t)=\|\psi_{t}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\psi_{t}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}.\end{split}

(3.24)

Assume throughout that
\psi(\cdot,t)
and
\psi_{t}(\cdot,t)
belong
C([0,\infty),L^{\infty}(\Gamma))
, hence
f(t)
and
\tilde{f}(t)
belong to
C([0,\infty))
.
Whenever
f^{\prime}(t)
is mentioned, we implicitly assume that
f\in C^{1}((0,\infty))
.
Let
M_{f}(t)
be a continuous, increasing majorant of
f(t)
on
[0,\infty)
.
Let

A=\limsup_{t\to\infty}f(t)\quad\text{and}\quad\beta=\limsup_{t\to\infty}[f^{\prime}(t)]^{-}.

(3.25)

Again, whenever
\beta
is used in subsequently statements,
it is understood that
f(t)\in C^{1}((0,\infty))
.

Also, for a function
u(x,t)
defined on
U\times[0,\infty)
we denote

J_{H}[u](t)=\int_{U}H(|\nabla u(x,t)|)dx,

(3.26)

where
H
is the function defined by (
2.16
). Note from (
2.17
) that

c_{1}\int_{U}|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2-a}dx-c_{3}\leq J_{H}[u](t)\leq c_{2}\int_{U}|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2-a}dx,\quad c_{3}>0.

(3.27)

We now recall relevant estimates from
[
18
]
.

Theorem 3.3

(cf.
[
18
]
, Theorem 4.3)
.

The following estimates hold

(i)

For
t\geq 0
,

\|{\bar{p}(t)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}J_{H}[p](\tau)d\tau\leq\|{\bar{p}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\int_{0}^{t}f(\tau)d\tau.

(3.28)

(ii)

Assume that the Degree Condition holds, then

\|{\bar{p}(t)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|{\bar{p}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C(1+M_{f}(t)^{\frac{2}{2-a}})\quad\text{for all }t\geq 0.

(3.29)

Moreover, if
A<\infty
then

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\|{\bar{p}(t)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C(A+A^{\frac{2}{2-a}}),

(3.30)

and if
\beta<\infty
then there is
T>0
such that

\|{\bar{p}(t)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\big(1+\beta^{\frac{1}{1-a}}+f(t)^{\frac{2}{2-a}}\big)\quad\text{for all }t>T.

(3.31)

We finalize our main estimates for
L^{\infty}
-norm in the following theorem.
The exponent
\mu_{2}
appearing below is already defined in (
3.22
), and we also denote

\mu_{4}=\frac{4}{(2-a)(4-a(n+2))}.

(3.32)

Theorem 3.4
.

Assume (
SDC
). Then there is a constant
C>0
such that the following statements hold true.

(i)

For
t>0
,

\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big(1+t^{-\frac{1}{\mu_{2}(2-a)}}\Big)\Big\{1+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}+M_{f}(t)^{\mu_{4}}\Big\},

(3.33)

\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big(1+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}+M_{f}(t)^{\mu_{4}}\Big).

(3.34)

(ii)

If
A<\infty
then

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\big(1+A^{\mu_{4}}\big).

(3.35)

(iii)

If
\beta<\infty
then there is
T>0
such that

\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big\{1+\beta^{\frac{\mu_{4}(2-a)}{2(1-a)}}+\sup_{[t-2,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{\mu_{4}(2-a)}{1-a}}\Big\}\quad\hbox{for all }t>T.

(3.36)

Proof.

(i) We prove (
3.33
) first.
For
t\in(0,1)
, applying (
3.13
) to
T_{0}=0
,
T=t
,
\delta=1
and
\theta=1/2
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big\{(t+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+\sup_{[0,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}})^{\frac{2-a}{2(1-a)}})

\displaystyle\quad+(t+1)^{\frac{1}{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}}\big(1+2t^{-1}\big)^{\frac{1}{(2-a)\mu_{2}}}\Big(\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(0,t))}+\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(0,t))}^{\frac{4}{4-a(n+2)}}\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+t^{-1})^{\frac{1}{(2-a)\mu_{2}}}\Big(1+\sup_{[0,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{2(1-a)}}+\sup_{[0,t]}\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{4}{4-a(n+2)}}\Big)\Big\}.

By estimate (
3.29
),

\displaystyle\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{\mu_{2}(2-a)}}\Big\{1+M_{f}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\Big(1+\|{\bar{p}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}+M_{f}(t)^{\frac{1}{2-a}}\Big)^{\frac{4}{4-a(n+2)}}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{\mu_{2}(2-a)}}\Big\{1+\|{\bar{p}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{4}{4-a(n+2)}}+M_{f}(t)^{\frac{4}{(2-a)(4-a(n+2))}}\Big\}.

Therefore, we obtain (
3.33
) for
t\in(0,1)
.
For
t\in[1,\infty)
, applying (
3.13
) to
T_{0}=t-1
,
T=1
,
\delta=1
, and
\theta=1/2
, we have

\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big\{1+\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{2(1-a)}}+\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|{\bar{p}}\|_{L^{2}}+\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|{\bar{p}(t)}\|_{L^{2}}^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}\Big\}.

Note that
\mu_{4}(2-a)>1
, then Hölder’s inequality gives

\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big\{1+\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{2(1-a)}}+\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|{\bar{p}}\|_{L^{2}}^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}\Big\}.

(3.37)

Then, again, combining (
3.37
) with (
3.29
) and using Hölder’s inequality yield (
3.33
) for
t\in[1,\infty)
.

To prove (
3.34
), we note that in the case
t\geq 1
, (
3.33
) implies (
3.34
).
Now, for
t\in(0,1),
we have from inequality (
3.9
) that

\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\big(1+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sup_{[0,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{1-a}}\big).

(3.38)

Since
\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq M_{f}(t)^{\frac{1-a}{2-a}}\leq M_{f}(t)^{\mu_{4}}
, then (
3.34
) follows (
3.38
).

(ii) Since
A<\infty
, we see that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}(\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}})^{\frac{2-a}{2(1-a)}}\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}f(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}=A^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(3.39)

It follows (
3.37
), (
3.30
) and (
3.39
) that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big\{1+A^{\frac{1}{2}}+(A^{\frac{1}{2}}+A^{\frac{1}{2-a}})^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}\Big\},

which implies (
3.35
).

(iii) Because
\beta<\infty
, using (
3.31
) in (
3.37
) there is
T>0
such that for all
t>T
,

\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big\{1+\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{2(1-a)}}+\big[1+\beta^{\frac{1}{2(1-a)}}+\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{1}{1-a}}\big]^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}\Big\},

and (
3.36
) follows.
∎

Concerning
\bar{p}(t)
being small, as
t\to\infty
, rather than just being bounded, we have the following result.

Theorem 3.5
.

Assume (
SDC
). For any
\varepsilon>0
, there is
\delta_{0}>0
such that

\text{if}\quad\limsup_{t\to\infty}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}<\delta_{0}\quad\text{then}\quad\limsup_{t\to\infty}\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}<\varepsilon.

(3.40)

Consequently,

\text{if}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\psi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}=0\quad\text{then}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}=0.

(3.41)

Proof.

Applying (
3.13
) to
T_{0}=t-1
,
T=1
and
\theta=1/2
, we have

\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big\{\sqrt{\mathcal{E}(t)}+(1+\delta^{-a})^{\frac{1}{(2-a)\mu_{2}}}\Big(\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(t-1,t))}+\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(t-1,t))}^{\frac{4}{4-a(n+2)}}\Big)\Big\},

where

\mathcal{E}(t)=\delta^{2-a}+\delta^{-a}\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\delta^{-\frac{a(2-a)}{1-a}}\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}.

Then, it follows that

\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\sqrt{\mathcal{E}(t)}+C(1+\delta^{-a})^{\frac{1}{(2-a)\mu_{2}}}\Big\{\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|{\bar{p}}\|_{L^{2}}+\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|{\bar{p}}\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{4}{4-a(n+2)}}\Big\}.

(3.42)

Let
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
, then

A\leq\delta_{0}^{2}+\delta_{0}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}\leq 2\delta_{0}^{2}
,
and therefore, by (
3.30
),

\limsup_{t\to\infty}(\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|{\bar{p}}\|_{L^{2}})\leq C(A^{\frac{1}{2}}+A^{\frac{1}{2-a}})\leq C\delta_{0}.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty}(1+\delta^{-a})^{\frac{1}{(2-a)\mu_{2}}}\Big\{\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|{\bar{p}}\|_{L^{2}}+\sup_{[t-1,t]}\|{\bar{p}}\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{4}{4-a(n+2)}}\Big\}\leq C(1+\delta^{-a})^{\frac{1}{(2-a)\mu_{2}}}\delta_{0}.

Also,

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{E}(t)\leq\delta^{2-a}+\delta^{-a}\delta_{0}^{2}+\delta^{-\frac{a(2-a)}{1-a}}\delta_{0}^{\frac{2-a}{1-a}}\leq\delta^{2-a}+2\delta^{-\frac{a(2-a)}{1-a}}\delta_{0}^{2}.

Therefore, for any
\delta>0
, we have from (
3.42
) that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\|\bar{p}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\big(\delta^{1-\frac{a}{2}}+[\delta^{-\frac{a(2-a)}{2(1-a)}}+(1+\delta^{-a})^{\frac{1}{(2-a)\mu_{2}}}]\delta_{0}\big).

(3.43)

Now, choose
\delta
sufficiently small such that
2C\delta^{1-a/2}<\varepsilon
. Then we can choose
\delta_{0}>0
even sufficiently smaller so that

[\delta^{-\frac{a(2-a)}{2(1-a)}}+(1+\delta^{-a})^{\frac{1}{(2-a)\mu_{2}}}]\delta_{0}<\delta^{1-a/2}.

From this, the desired estimates (
3.40
) follows (
3.43
).
The statement (
3.41
) obviously is a consequence of (
3.40
). The proof is complete.
∎

3.2.
Estimates for pressure’s gradient

In this subsection, we establish the interior
L^{s}
-estimate of
\nabla p
, for all
s>0
.
We follow the approach in
[
20
]
.
First is a basic estimate for
\nabla p
which prepares for our iteration later.

Lemma 3.6
.

For each
s\geq 0
, there is a constant
C>0
depending on
s
such that for any
T>0
and smooth cut-off function

\zeta(x)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
, the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\nabla p(x,t)|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx+C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla^{2}p|^{2}|\nabla p|^{2s}\zeta^{2}dxdt

(3.44)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx+C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2s+2}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}dxdt.

Proof.

In this proof, we use Einstein’s summation convention, that is, when an index variable appears twice in a single term it implies summation of that term over all the values of the index.
Multiplying the equation (
3.1
) by

-\nabla\cdot(|\nabla p|^{2s}\nabla p\zeta^{2})
and integrating the resultant over
U
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2s+2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\nabla p|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx=-\int_{U}\partial_{i}(K(|\nabla p|)\partial_{i}p)\,\partial_{j}(|\nabla p|^{2s}\partial_{j}p\zeta^{2})dx.

This equality and the integration by parts yield

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2s+2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\nabla p|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx=-\int_{U}\partial_{j}[K(|\nabla p|)\partial_{i}p]\,\partial_{i}[|\nabla p|^{2s}\partial_{j}p\zeta^{2}]dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{U}\partial_{j}[K(|\nabla p|)\partial_{i}p]\,\partial_{i}\partial_{j}p\,|\nabla p|^{2s}\zeta^{2}dx-\int_{U}\partial_{j}[K(|\nabla p|)\partial_{i}p]\,\partial_{j}p\,|\nabla p|^{2s}\partial_{i}[\zeta^{2}]dx

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{U}\partial_{j}[K(|\nabla p|)\partial_{i}p]\,\partial_{j}p\,[2s|\nabla p|^{2s-2}\partial_{i}\partial_{m}p\partial_{m}p]\,\zeta^{2}dx.

We rewrite it in the following form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2s+2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\nabla p|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx=-\int_{U}\Big[\partial_{y_{l}}(K(|y|)y_{i})\Big|_{y=\nabla p}\partial_{j}\partial_{l}p\Big]\partial_{j}\partial_{i}p|\nabla p|^{2s}\zeta^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{U}\Big[\partial_{y_{l}}(K(|y|)y_{i})\Big|_{y=\nabla p}\partial_{j}\partial_{l}p\Big]\partial_{j}p\,|\nabla p|^{2s}\,\zeta\partial_{i}\zeta dx

\displaystyle\quad-2s\int_{U}\Big[\partial_{y_{l}}(K(|y|)y_{i})\Big|_{y=\nabla p}\partial_{j}\partial_{l}p\Big]\partial_{j}p\,(|\nabla p|^{2s-2}\partial_{i}\partial_{m}p\partial_{m}p)\,\zeta^{2}dx.

We denote the three terms on the right-hand side by
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
, and estimate each of them.
By (
2.11
) one can easily prove for any
y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that

z^{T}\nabla(K(|y|)y)z\geq(1-a)K(|y|)|z|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|\nabla(K(|y|)y)|\leq(1+a)K(|y|).

It follows that

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=-\int_{U}\partial_{y_{l}}[K(|y|)y_{i}]\Big|_{y=\nabla p}\partial_{l}(\partial_{j}p)\partial_{i}(\partial_{j}p)\,|\nabla p|^{2s}\zeta^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq-(1-a)\sum_{j}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla(\partial_{j}p)|^{2}|\nabla p|^{2s}\zeta^{2}dx

\displaystyle=-(1-a)\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla^{2}p|^{2}\,|\nabla p|^{2s}\zeta^{2}dx.

Moreover, we have

|I_{2}|\leq 2(1+a)\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla^{2}p||\nabla p|^{2s+1}\zeta|\nabla\zeta|dx,

and

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=-2s\int_{U}\partial_{y_{l}}[K(|y|)y_{i}]\Big|_{y=\nabla p}(\partial_{l}\partial_{j}p\partial_{j}p)(\partial_{i}\partial_{m}p\partial_{m}p)\,|\nabla p|^{2s-2}\zeta^{2}dx

\displaystyle=-2s\int_{U}\partial_{y_{l}}[K(|y|)y_{i}]\Big|_{y=\nabla p}\Big(\partial_{l}\frac{1}{2}|\nabla p|^{2}\Big)\Big(\partial_{i}\frac{1}{2}|\nabla p|^{2}\Big)\,|\nabla p|^{2s-2}\zeta^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq-2(1-a)s\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)\Big|\nabla\Big(\frac{1}{2}|\nabla p|^{2}\Big)\Big|^{2}|\nabla p|^{2s-2}\zeta^{2}dx\leq 0.

Combining these estimates together with Young’s inequality, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2s+2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\nabla p|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx+(1-a)\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla^{2}p|^{2}|\nabla p|^{2s}\zeta^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+a)\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla^{2}p||\nabla p|^{2s+1}\zeta|\nabla\zeta|dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-a}{2}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla^{2}p|^{2}|\nabla p|^{2s}\zeta^{2}dx+C\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2s+2}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}dx.

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2s+2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\nabla p|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx+\frac{1-a}{2}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla^{2}p|^{2}|\nabla p|^{2s}\zeta^{2}dx

(3.45)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2s+2}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}dx.

Integrating this inequality in time yields (
3.44
).
∎

In order to iterate (
3.44
) in
s
we need an embedding similar to Lemma 5.4 on page 93 in
[
20
]
.
For our degenerate equation, the following version has the key weight function
K(|\nabla w|)
.

Lemma 3.7
.

For each
s\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
s
such that for each smooth cut-off function

\zeta(x)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
, the following inequality holds

\displaystyle\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx\leq C\sup_{\text{\rm supp}\zeta}|w|^{2}

\displaystyle\left\{\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s-2}|\nabla^{2}w|^{2}\zeta^{2}dx\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}dx\right\},

for every sufficiently regular function
w(x)
such that the right hand side is well-defined.

Proof.

Again, the Einstein’s summation convention is used in this proof.

Let
I=\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx
.
From direct calculations, we see that

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}\partial_{i}w\partial_{i}w\zeta^{2}dx=-\int_{U}\partial_{i}(K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}\partial_{i}w\zeta^{2})\cdot wdx

\displaystyle=-\int_{U}\Big(K^{\prime}(|\nabla w|)\frac{\partial_{i}\partial_{j}w\partial_{j}w}{|\nabla w|}\Big)|\nabla w|^{2s}\partial_{i}w\cdot\zeta^{2}\cdot wdx-\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}\Delta w\cdot\zeta^{2}\cdot wdx

\displaystyle\quad-2s\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)\Big(|\nabla w|^{2s-2}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}w\partial_{j}w\Big)\cdot\partial_{i}w\cdot\zeta^{2}\cdot wdx-2\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}\partial_{i}w\zeta\partial_{i}\zeta\cdot wdx.

From this and (
2.11
), it follows that

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\leq a\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla^{2}w||\nabla w|^{2s}\zeta^{2}|w|dx+\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}|\Delta w|\zeta^{2}|w|dx

\displaystyle\quad+2s\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s-2}|\nabla^{2}w||\nabla w|^{2}\zeta^{2}|w|dx+2\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}|\nabla w|\zeta|\nabla\zeta||w|dx

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}|\nabla^{2}w|\zeta^{2}|w|dx+C\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s+1}\zeta|\nabla\zeta||w|dx.

This last inequality and the Young’s inequality imply that

I\leq\frac{1}{2}I+C\Big\{\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s-2}|\nabla^{2}w|^{2}\zeta^{2}|w|^{2}dx+\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}|w|^{2}dx\Big\}.

Therefore, we obtain

I\leq C\sup_{{\rm supp}\zeta}|w|^{2}\Big\{\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s-2}|\nabla^{2}w|^{2}\zeta^{2}dx+\int_{U}K(|\nabla w|)|\nabla w|^{2s}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}dx\Big\}.

This completes the proof.
∎

We combine Lemmas
3.6
and
3.7
now. By applying Lemma
3.7
to
\bar{p}
with
s+1
in place of
s
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2s+4}\zeta^{2}dxdt\leq C\sup_{\rm{supp}\zeta}|\bar{p}|^{2}

\displaystyle\left\{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2s}|\nabla^{2}p|^{2}\zeta^{2}dxdt\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2s+2}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}dxdt\right\}.

This last inequality and (
3.44
) imply for
s\geq 0
that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2s+4}\zeta^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{{\rm supp}\zeta}|\bar{p}|^{2}\left[\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2s+2}\zeta^{2}dx\right.

(3.46)

\displaystyle+\left.\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2s+2}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}dxdt\right].

Also, from (
3.44
), we have for
s\geq 2
that

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\nabla p(x,t)|^{s}\zeta^{2}dx\leq\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s}\zeta^{2}dx+C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}dxdt.

(3.47)

We are ready to iterate the relation (
3.46
). Hereafterward, we consider
U^{\prime}\Subset U
, that is,
U^{\prime}
is an open set compactly contained in
U
.

Proposition 3.8
.

For
U^{\prime}\Subset V\subset U
and
s\geq 2
there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
U^{\prime}
,
V
and
s
such that for any
T>0
, we have

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq C\Big(1+\sup_{[0,T]}\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{\infty}(V)}\Big)^{s-2}\\
&\quad\cdot\Big\{\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2}dx+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s-2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2}dxdt\Big\}.\end{split}

(3.48)

Proof.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N},m\geq 2
be a fixed number. Then, let
\{U_{k}\}_{k=0}^{m}
be a family of open, smooth domains in
U
such that

U^{\prime}=U_{m}\Subset U_{m-1}\Subset U_{m-2}\Subset\ldots\Subset U_{1}\Subset U_{0}=V\subset U
. For each
k=1,2,\cdots,m
, let
\zeta_{k}(x)
be a smooth cut-off function
which is equal to one on
U_{k}
and zero on
U\setminus U_{k-1}
. There is a positive constant
C>0
depending on
\zeta_{k}
,
k=1,2,\ldots,m
, such that

|\nabla\zeta_{k}|\leq C
, for all
k=1,2,\cdots,m.

Also, for each integer
k\geq 1
and
s_{0}\geq 0
, we define

X_{k}=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U_{k}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2k+s_{0}}dxdt,\quad A_{k}=\int_{U_{k}}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2k+s_{0}}dx.

Then, by applying (
3.46
) with
\zeta=\zeta_{k}
and
2s+2=2k+s_{0}
, we see that

X_{k+1}\leq C_{k,m,s_{0}}N_{0}(A_{k}+X_{k}),\quad k=1,2,\cdots m-1,

(3.49)

where
N_{0}=\sup_{V\times[0,T]}|\bar{p}|^{2}=\sup_{[0,T]}\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{\infty}(V)}^{2}
.
Letting
C_{m}=\max\{C_{k,m,s_{0}}:k=1,2,\ldots,m-1\}
, we have from (
3.49
) that

X_{k+1}\leq C_{m}N_{0}(A_{k}+X_{k}),\quad k=1,2,\cdots m-1,

This inequality particularly yields

\begin{split}X_{m}&\leq(C_{m}N_{0})A_{m-1}+(C_{m}N_{0})^{2}A_{m-2}+\cdots+(C_{m}N_{0})^{m-1}A_{1}+(C_{m}N_{0})^{m-1}X_{1}\\
&\leq C[(N_{0}A_{m-1}+N_{0}^{2}A_{m-2}+\cdots+N_{0}^{m-1}A_{1})+N_{0}^{m-1}X_{1}],\end{split}

with
C=C(m)
depending on all sets
U_{k}
and functions
\zeta_{k}
, for
k=1,2,\ldots,m-1
.
In other words, we have proved that for each integer
m\geq 2
and real number
s\geq 0
, there is
C=C(m,s_{0})
such that

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2m+s_{0}}dxdt&\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}N_{0}^{i}\int_{U_{m-i}}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2(m-i)+s_{0}}dx\\
&\quad+CN_{0}^{m-1}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U_{1}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2+s_{0}}dxdt.\end{split}

By using Young’s inequality, one can rewrite this inequality as

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2m+s_{0}}dxdt\leq C(N_{0}+N_{0}^{m-1})\\
\cdot\Big\{\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2+s_{0}}dx+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2m+s_{0}-2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U_{1}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2+s_{0}}dxdt\Big\}.

(3.50)

In particular, with
m=2
and
s_{0}=0
, (
3.50
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{4}dx

\displaystyle\leq CN_{0}\Big\{\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U_{1}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2}dxdt\Big\}.

(3.51)

This implies (
3.48
) when
s=4
. In case
s\in(2,4)
, let
\alpha
and
\beta
be two positive numbers such that

\frac{1}{s}=\frac{\alpha}{2}+\frac{\beta}{4}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha+\beta=1.

(3.52)

Then, using interpolation inequality, we get

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\Big)^{\frac{1}{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U_{1}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2}dxdt\Big)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{4}dxdt\Big)^{\frac{\beta}{4}}

From this and (
3.51
), it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq CN_{0}^{\frac{\beta s}{4}}\Big\{\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U_{1}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2}dx\Big\}.

From (
3.52
) follows
\beta s/4=s/2-1
. Thus, we have

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq CN_{0}^{\frac{s}{2}-1}\Big\{\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2}dx\Big\}.

This implies (
3.48
) for
s\in(2,4)
. Therefore, we have proved (
3.48
) with
s\in(2,4]
. Now, for
s>4
, there are a number
s_{0}\in(0,2]
and an integer
m\geq 2
such that
s=s_{0}+2m
. From (
3.50
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq C(N_{0}+N_{0}^{m-1})\Big\{\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2+s_{0}}dx+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2m+s_{0}-2}dx

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U_{1}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2+s_{0}}dxdt\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq C(N_{0}+N_{0}^{\frac{s-s_{0}-2}{2}})\Big\{\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2+s_{0}}dx+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s-2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U_{1}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2+s_{0}}dxdt\Big\}.

Since
s_{0}+2\in(2,4]
, it follows from the last inequality and estimate (
3.48
) already proved for the case
s\in(2,4]
with
U_{1}
in place of
U^{\prime}
and
s_{0}+2
in place of
s
that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq C(N_{0}+N_{0}^{\frac{s-s_{0}-2}{2}})\Big\{\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2+s_{0}}dx+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s-2}dx

\displaystyle\quad+N_{0}^{\frac{s_{0}}{2}}\Big(\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2}dxdt\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+N_{0}^{\frac{s-2}{2}})\Big\{\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2}dx+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s-2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2}dxdt\Big\}.

From this, the desired estimate (
3.48
) follows and the proof is complete.
∎

Now, we give specific estimates for the
L^{s}
-norm of
\nabla p
in terms of initial and boundary data.
We denote

\mathcal{K}_{1,T}=1+\sup_{[0,T]}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(3.53)

Theorem 3.9
.

Assume (
SDC
). Then for
s\geq 2
, there is a positive constant
C
depending on
U^{\prime}
and
s
such that for any
T>0

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq CL_{1}(s)(T+1)^{\frac{2(s-2)}{2-a}+1}\mathcal{K}_{1,T}^{\frac{s-a}{1-a}},

(3.54)

\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p(x,t)|^{s}dx\leq CL_{2}(s)(T+1)^{\frac{2(s-2)}{2-a}+1}\mathcal{K}_{1,T}^{\frac{s-a}{1-a}},

(3.55)

where

\displaystyle L_{1}(s)

\displaystyle=L_{1}(s;[p_{0}])\mathbin{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbin{=\kern-2.0pt=}}\limits^{\rm def}}}(1+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})^{s-2}\Big(1+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{\max\{2,s-2\}}dx\Big),

\displaystyle L_{2}(s)

\displaystyle=L_{2}(s;[p_{0}])\mathbin{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbin{=\kern-2.0pt=}}\limits^{\rm def}}}(1+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}})^{s-2}\Big(1+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s}dx\Big).

Proof.

From the inequality (
3.9
) in Proposition
3.1
, it follows that

\sup_{[0,T]}\|\bar{p}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\Big\{\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+(T+1)^{\frac{2}{2-a}}\mathcal{K}_{1,T}^{\frac{1}{1-a}}\Big\}.

Thanks to (
3.28
) of Theorem
3.3
, we have

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{2}dxdt\leq\|{\bar{p}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\int_{0}^{T}f(\tau)d\tau.

(3.56)

These inequalities together with (
3.48
) of Proposition
3.8
yield

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq C\Big(\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+(T+1)^{\frac{2}{2-a}}\mathcal{K}_{1,T}^{\frac{1}{1-a}}\Big)^{s-2}\\
\cdot\Big(\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{U}\big(|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{2}+|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s-2}\big)dx+\int_{0}^{T}f(\tau)d\tau\Big).

Applying Poincaré’s inequality to
\bar{p}_{0}
, and using Young’s inequality, we obtain

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq C\Big(\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+(T+1)^{\frac{2}{2-a}}\mathcal{K}_{1,T}^{\frac{1}{1-a}}\Big)^{s-2}\\
\cdot\Big(1+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{\max\{2,s-2\}}dx+\int_{0}^{T}f(\tau)d\tau\Big),

(3.57)

Then (
3.54
) follows.
It follows from (
3.47
) and (
3.54
) that

\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p(x,t)|^{s}dx\leq\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s}dx+C\Big(\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+(T+1)^{\frac{2}{2-a}}\mathcal{K}_{1,T}^{\frac{1}{1-a}}\Big)^{s-2}\\
\cdot\Big(1+\int_{U}\big(|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{\max\{2,s-2\}}dx+\int_{0}^{T}f(\tau)d\tau\Big),

Then by Young’s inequality we obtain

\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p(x,t)|^{s}dx\leq C\Big(\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+(T+1)^{\frac{2}{2-a}}\mathcal{K}_{1,T}^{\frac{1}{1-a}}\Big)^{s-2}\\
\cdot\Big(1+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s}dx+\int_{0}^{T}f(\tau)d\tau\Big).

(3.58)

Then (
3.55
) follows.
∎

Recall that
f
is defined in (
3.24
) and
M_{f}
is a continuous increasing majorant of
f
. Our next result is similar to Theorem
3.9
but the estimates do not contain the power growth in
T
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Assume (
SDC
). For
s\geq 2
, there is a positive constant
C
depending on
U^{\prime}
and
s
such that for any
T>1
we have

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq CL_{3}(s)\Big(1+M_{f}(T)\Big)^{\mu_{4}(s-2)}\Big\{1+\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dt\Big\},

(3.59)

\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p(x,t)|^{s}dx\leq CL_{4}(s)\Big(1+M_{f}(T)\Big)^{\mu_{4}(s-2)}\Big\{1+\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dt\Big\},

(3.60)

where

\displaystyle L_{3}(s)

\displaystyle=L_{3}(s;[p_{0}])\mathbin{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbin{=\kern-2.0pt=}}\limits^{\rm def}}}\Big(1+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}\Big)^{s-2}\Big\{1+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{\max\{2,s-2\}}dx\Big\},

\displaystyle L_{4}(s)

\displaystyle=L_{4}(s;[p_{0}])\mathbin{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbin{=\kern-2.0pt=}}\limits^{\rm def}}}\Big(1+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}\Big)^{s-2}\Big\{1+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s}dx\Big\}.

Proof.

The proof is the same as in Theorem
3.9
with the use of estimate (
3.34
) in place of (
3.9
).
Instead of (
3.54
) and (
3.55
), we have, respectively,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(1+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}+M_{f}(T)^{\mu_{4}}\Big)^{s-2}

(3.61)

\displaystyle\cdot\Big\{1+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{\max\{2,s-2\}}dx+\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dt\Big\},

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p(x,t)|^{s}dx

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(1+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\bar{p}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{\mu_{4}(2-a)}+M_{f}(T)^{\mu_{4}}\Big)^{s-2}

(3.62)

\displaystyle\cdot\Big\{1+\int_{U}|\nabla p_{0}(x)|^{s}dx+\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dt\Big\}.

Then (
3.59
) and (
3.60
) follow, respectively.
∎

In (
3.59
) and (
3.60
), letting
T\to\infty
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 3.11
.

Under the Strict Degree Condition, if

\Upsilon_{1}\mathbin{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbin{=\kern-2.0pt=}}\limits^{\rm def}}}1+\sup_{[0,\infty)}f<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\Upsilon_{2}\mathbin{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathbin{=\kern-2.0pt=}}\limits^{\rm def}}}1+\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t)dt<\infty,

(3.63)

then for any
s\geq 2
, there is a constant
C=C(U^{\prime},s)>0
such that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq CL_{3}(s)\Upsilon_{1}^{\mu_{4}(s-2)}\Upsilon_{2},

(3.64)

\sup_{[0,\infty)}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p(x,t)|^{s}dx\leq CL_{4}(s)\Upsilon_{1}^{\mu_{4}(s-2)}\Upsilon_{2}.

(3.65)

Using the preceding theorems and the properties of function
K(\cdot)
, we can derive the following direct estimates for
\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt
.

Theorem 3.12
.

Assume (
SDC
). For
s\geq 2-a
, there is
a constant
C>0
such that for any
T>0
we have

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq C\leq CL_{1}(s+a)(T+1)^{\frac{2s}{2-a}-1}\mathcal{K}_{1,T}^{\frac{s}{1-a}},

(3.66)

and, alternatively,

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq CT+CL_{3}(s+a)\Big(1+M_{f}(T)\Big)^{\mu_{4}(s+a-2)}\Big\{1+\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dt\Big\},

(3.67)

where the positive numbers
L_{1}(\cdot)
and
L_{3}(\cdot)
are defined in Theorems
3.9
and
3.10
.

Proof.

By relation (
2.14
),

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}|\nabla p|^{s}dxdt\leq CT+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U^{\prime}}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla p|^{s+a}dxdt.

(3.68)

The last integral is estimated by applying (
3.54
) with
s+a
replacing
s
.
As a result, we obtain (
3.66
).
Now, using relation (
3.68
) and applying (
3.59
) for
s+a
in place of
s
, we obtain (
3.67
).
∎

3.3.
Estimates for pressure’s time derivative

In this subsection, we derive the interior
L^{\infty}
-norm of
\bar{p}_{t}
.
Throughout this subsection
U^{\prime}\Subset U
.
Under the Strict Degree Condition, let

{\mu_{5}}=4\Big(1-\frac{1}{(2-a)^{*}}\Big),\quad\mu_{6}=1-\frac{2}{\mu_{5}},\quad\mu_{7}=\frac{2}{{\mu_{5}}(2-a)}.

(3.69)

Then
{\mu_{5}}\in(2,(2-a)^{*})
and
\mu_{6},\mu_{7}\in(0,1).

Proposition 3.13
.

Assume (
SDC
).
There is a constant
C=C(U^{\prime})>0
such that for any
T_{0}\geq 0
,
T>0
and
\theta\in(0,1)
, we have

\sup_{[T_{0}+\theta T,T_{0}+T]}\|\bar{p}_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(U^{\prime})}\leq C\Big\{\big[\lambda(1+(\theta T)^{-1/2})\big]^{\frac{1}{\mu_{6}}}+\big[\lambda T^{1/2}\sup_{[T_{0},T_{0}+T]}\|\psi_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big]^{\frac{1}{\mu_{6}+1}}\Big\}\\
\cdot\Big(\|\bar{p}_{t}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(T_{0},T_{0}+T))}+\|\bar{p}_{t}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(T_{0},T_{0}+T))}^{\frac{\mu_{6}}{\mu_{6}+1}}\Big),

(3.70)

where

\lambda=1+\sup_{[T_{0},T_{0}+T]}\left(\int_{U}|\nabla p(x,t)|^{2-a}dx\right)^{\mu_{7}},

and

\sup_{[T_{0}+\theta T,T_{0}+T]}\|p_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(U^{\prime})}\leq C\Big(1+\frac{1}{\theta T}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\mu_{6}}}\Big(1+\sup_{[T_{0},T_{0}+T]}\|{\nabla p}\|_{L^{2-a}(U)}^{\frac{2}{{\mu_{5}}-2}}\Big)\|{p_{t}}\|_{L^{2}(U\times(T_{0},T_{0}+T))}.

(3.71)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
T_{0}=0
.
We prove (
3.70
) first.
Let
q=p_{t}
and

\bar{q}=q-\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{U}qdx=p_{t}-\frac{1}{|U|}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}pdx=p_{t}+\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{\Gamma}\psi(x,t)d\sigma=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}=\bar{p}_{t}.

Then it follows from (
3.1
) that
\bar{q}
solves

\frac{\partial\bar{q}}{\partial t}=\nabla\cdot\big(K(|\nabla p|)\nabla p\big)_{t}+\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{\Gamma}\psi_{t}d\sigma.

(3.72)

For
k\geq 0
, let
\bar{q}^{(k)}=\max\{\bar{q}-k,0\}

and
\chi_{k}(x,t)
be the characteristic function of set
\{(x,t)\in U\times(0,T):\bar{q}(x,t)>k\}
.
On
S_{k}(t)
, we have
(\nabla p)_{t}=(\nabla\bar{p})_{t}=\nabla\bar{q}=\nabla\bar{q}^{(k)}
.

Let
\zeta=\zeta(x,t)
be the cut-off function on
U\times[0,T]
satisfying
\zeta(\cdot,0)=0
and
\zeta(\cdot,t)
having compact support in
U
.
We will use test function
\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta^{2}
, noting that
\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta^{2})=\zeta[\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)+\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta]
.

Multiplying (
3.72
) by
\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta^{2}
and integrating the resultant on
U
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta\zeta_{t}dx-\int_{U}(K(|\nabla p|))_{t}\nabla p\cdot[\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)+\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta]\zeta dx

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)(\nabla p)_{t}\cdot[\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)+\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta\nabla]\zeta dx+\frac{1}{|U|}\int_{\Gamma}\psi_{t}d\sigma\int_{U}\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta^{2}dx.

Put
z=\zeta[\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)+\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta]
.
We simplify the third term on the right-hand side of the last inequality as

(\nabla p)_{t}\cdot z=\zeta\nabla\bar{q}^{(k)}\cdot[\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)+\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta]\\
=[\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)-\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta]\cdot[\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)+\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta]=|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|^{2}-|\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta|^{2}.

For the second term of right-hand side, using (
2.11
) we have

\displaystyle|(K(|\nabla p|))_{t}\nabla p\cdot z|\

\displaystyle=|K^{\prime}(|\nabla p|)|\frac{|\nabla p\cdot\nabla p_{t}|}{|\nabla p|}|\nabla p\cdot z|\leq aK(|\nabla p|)|\nabla\bar{q}||z|.

Moreover,

\displaystyle|\nabla\bar{q}||z|

\displaystyle=|\zeta\nabla\bar{q}^{(k)}||\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)+q^{(k)}\nabla\zeta|\leq(|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|+|\bar{q}^{(k)}||\nabla\zeta|)^{2}

\displaystyle=|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|^{2}+2|\bar{q}^{(k)}||\nabla\zeta||\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|+|\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta|^{2}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}dx+(1-a)\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta|\zeta_{t}|dx+(1+a)\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad+2a\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta||\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|dx+C\|\psi_{t}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}|\zeta^{2}dx.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. By Cauchy’s inequality,

\displaystyle 2aK(|\nabla p|)|\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta||\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-a}{2}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|^{2}+\frac{2a^{2}}{1-a}K(|\nabla p|)|\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta|^{2},

\displaystyle C\|\psi_{t}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}|\bar{q}^{(k)}|\zeta^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}+C\varepsilon^{-1}\|\psi_{t}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\zeta^{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}dx+\frac{1-a}{2}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|^{2}dx\leq\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta|\zeta_{t}|dx

\displaystyle\quad+C\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta|^{2}dx+\varepsilon\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}dx+C\varepsilon^{-1}\|\psi_{t}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{U}\chi_{k}\zeta^{2}dx.

Now, integrating this inequality in time from
0
to
t
and taking supremum on
[0,T]
, we obtain

\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|^{2}dxdt\\
\leq C\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}|^{2}\zeta|\zeta_{t}|dxdt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\bar{q}^{(k)}\nabla\zeta|^{2}dxdt\Big]\\
+C_{1}\varepsilon T\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}dx+C\varepsilon^{-1}\sup_{[0,T]}\|\psi_{t}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}\chi_{k}\zeta^{2}dxdt.

Using the fact that the functions
K
and
\zeta
are bounded and taking
\varepsilon=1/(2C_{1}T)
, we obtain

\begin{split}&\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|^{2}dxdt\\
&\quad\leq C\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}|^{2}(\zeta|\zeta_{t}|+|\nabla\zeta|^{2})dxdt\right]+CT\sup_{[0,T]}\|\psi_{t}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}\chi_{k}\zeta^{2}dxdt.\end{split}

(3.73)

Applying inequality (
2.30
) in Lemma
2.4
to
\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta
with the weight
W(x,t)=K(|\nabla p(x,t)|)
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta}\|_{L^{\mu_{5}}(Q_{T})}\leq C\left\{\sup_{[0,T]}\Big(\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)^{-\frac{2-a}{a}}dx\Big)^{\frac{2}{s(2-a)}}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\left\{\sup_{[0,T]}\Big(\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}dx\Big)^{1/2}+\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|^{2}dxdt\Big)^{1/2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{[0,T]}\Big(\int_{U}(1+|\nabla p|)^{2-a}dx\Big)^{\mu_{7}}\cdot\left\{\sup_{[0,T]}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta|^{2}dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}K(|\nabla p|)|\nabla(\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta)|^{2}dxdt\right\}^{1/2}.

Therefore, by (
3.73
)

\|{\bar{q}^{(k)}\zeta}\|_{L^{\mu_{5}}(Q_{T})}\leq C\lambda\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}|\bar{q}^{(k)}|^{2}(|\zeta_{t}|\zeta+|\nabla\zeta|^{2})dxdt+T\sup_{[0,T]}\|\psi_{t}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}\chi_{k}\zeta^{2}dxdt\Big)^{1/2}.

(3.74)

Let
x_{0}
be any given point in
U
. Denote
\rho={\rm dist}(x_{0},\partial U)>0
.
Let
M_{0}>0
be fixed which will be determined later.
For
i\geq 0
, define

k_{i}=M_{0}(1-2^{-i}),\quad t_{i}=\theta T(1-2^{-i}),\quad\rho_{i}=\frac{1}{4}\rho(1+2^{-i}).

Then

t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\ldots<\theta T
and
\rho_{0}=\rho/2>\rho_{1}>\ldots>\rho/4>0.

Note that

\lim_{i\to\infty}t_{i}=\theta T\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{i\to\infty}\rho_{i}=\rho/4.

Let
U_{i}=\{x:\|{x-x_{0}}\|<\rho_{i}\}
then
U_{i+1}\Subset U_{i}
for
i=0,1,2,\ldots
.
For
i,j\geq 0
, we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{i}

\displaystyle=\{(x,t):x\in U_{i},\ t\in(t_{i},T)\},\quad A_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\{(x,t)\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}:\bar{q}(x,t)>k_{i},\ t\in(t_{j},T)\}.

(3.75)

For each
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, we use a cut-off function
\zeta_{i}(x,t)
which is piecewise linear in
t
and satisfies
\zeta_{i}\equiv 1
on
\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}
